### 3.2.2. Description of selected studies

Figure 2.6: Study area and geographical distribution of the papers selected in the “adaptation” corpus. The study area is composed of Europe and climatically similar regions. We used the Köppen-Geiger climate classification updated by Kottek et al., (2006) and the script provided by Rubel et al., (2017) to build the maps. [SOURCE: Page_70]

Fifty-three papers assessed the effect of adaptation on grain legume performances. Among them, only 13% were located in Europe, and 11% were global studies providing data for Europe (Table 2.2a). The remaining 76% were located in climatically similar regions and were divided between North America (51%), southern Brazil (8%), Iran (9%), and Australia (8%) [SOURCE: Page_70]

69 [SOURCE: Page_70]

(Figure 2.6). Soybean was studied in 77% of papers, followed by field pea (9%) and chickpea (8%) (Table 2.1). Only process-based and statistical models were used for these simulations (Table 2.2b). [SOURCE: Page_71]

Unlike the “impact” corpus, the first half of the century was studied more than the second with respect to both soybean and other grain legumes (Figure 2.2b). RCP$_4$.5 and RCP$_8$.5 remained the most studied climate scenarios, being used in 60% of papers (Table 2.2c). All studies accounted for changes in temperature and rainfall, and 62% of them considered the effect of CO$_2$ (Table 2.2d). Only one paper accounted for biotic stresses, and none of the selected studies considered the effect of ozone. [SOURCE: Page_71]

The effect of adaptation was measured on grain yield in 91% of papers, with 62% of them providing absolute grain yield data and 38% relative changes (Table 2.2e). Similar to the “impact” corpus, studies assessing the effect of adaptation on yield variability were scarce (2 papers). Numerous studies investigated the effect of irrigation, which explains the higher number of results on crop water use efficiency (19%), future water demand (13%), and water availability (4%) (Table 2.2f). Some variables assessed in the “adaptation” corpus were not considered in the “impact” corpus, for example greenhouse gas emissions (8%), soil organic matter content, or soil erosion (17%). These new variables suggest that adaptation strategies are often assessed for their contribution to both climate change adaptation and mitigation. Economic indicators remained scarce (2 papers). [SOURCE: Page_71]

Crop management options were studied in 53% of papers, and soil and water management was studied in 62% (Figure 2.7). Irrigation was the most studied technical option (40%), followed by modified sowing dates (23%), cultivar choice (19%), and virtual genotype (13%). Combinations of several adaptation strategies were seldom assessed (26% of selected studies), and little variety was found in the choice of the strategies combined (changes in sowing date and cultivar choice represented 64% of the combinations tested). Results on the effect of adaptation mostly apply to soybean, as few data were available for other grain legumes. Faba bean and lupins are extremely underrepresented, with only one paper found for each species. [SOURCE: Page_71]

70 [SOURCE: Page_71]

Figure 2.7: Number of papers per crop species and adaptation strategy. The size of the circle is proportional to the number of papers. Light grey circles represent the total number of papers and dark grey circles the number of studies focusing on Europe. A same paper can appear in several categories. [SOURCE: Page_72]

### 3.2.3. Crop management

Shifting cultivars and sowing dates were assessed for soybean in a majority of cases (76% of papers), yet data were also available for field pea (12%) and chickpea (12%) (Figure 2.7). Although the effect of these two strategies was neutral or positive in all studies, its magnitude varied significantly among locations and species (from 4% to more than 100% yield increase) (Figure 2.8). Combining shifts in cultivars and changes in sowing dates appeared as the most efficient strategy, with yield gains higher than 20%. Benefits were found not only on mean yield but also on yield stability (Falconnier et al., 2020) and pest management (Salam et al., 2011). In most studies, the choice of the optimal cultivars and sowing dates was site-specific (Bao et al., 2015a; Nendel et al., 2023; van Versendaal et al., 2023). For example, early-sown soybean in Canada (Jing et al., 2017) and south-eastern Europe (Minoli et al., 2022) benefitted from a longer growing season and higher yields, while late-sown soybean in the USA was found more capable of avoiding heat and water stresses (Bao et al., 2015b, 2015a). [SOURCE: Page_72]

71 [SOURCE: Page_72]

Figure 2.8: Effect of different adaptation strategies on crop yields for soybean (left) and other grain legumes (right). For each paper, the effect of adaptation is calculated as the ratio of the yield with adaptation to the yield without adaptation (expressed in %) and averaged over all time periods, climate scenarios, climate models and locations considered in that same paper (see Methods for details). Due to methodological differences and data availability, only 34 papers of the “adaptation” corpus were included here (see Appendix A, Table A.3). [SOURCE: Page_73]

The crop cycle length was often studied as a key parameter of virtual genotypes which could maintain or increase yields under climate change (Fu et al., 2016; Jing et al., 2017; Minoli et al., 2022; Osborne et al., 2013; Ravasi et al., 2020; Soltani and Sinclair, 2012). In several studies, extending the length of the growing period, and especially the grain filling period (Fu et al., 2016), was found to offset the negative impact of elevated temperature on crop cycle duration and soybean yields (Jing et al., 2017; Minoli et al., 2022). Conversely, in some areas where water and thermal stresses were frequent (e.g., southern France), an early maturity was found to be preferable for this crop (Minoli et al., 2022). A reduced vegetative phase allowing early flowering also gave positive results for field pea in Italy (Ravasi et al., 2020) and chickpea in Iran (Soltani and Sinclair, 2012). Other crop parameters were also explored, such as maintaining the harvest index to current levels for soybean (Jing et al., 2017) and widening the optimal temperature range for field pea (Ravasi et al., 2020). Increased drought resistance was obtained for soybean by manipulating water-related traits (rooting depth, transpiration function) (Battisti et al., 2017) or irradiating seeds with gamma rays (Beiranvand and Ghamghami, 2022). [SOURCE: Page_73]

Other crop management options tested included double-cropping and changes in sowing density. Climate change in the USA alleviated phenological constraints and allowed for expanding winter wheat-soybean double-cropping (Seifert and Lobell, 2015), especially in areas where the suitability of a corn-soybean rotation was predicted to decline (Lant et al., [SOURCE: Page_73]

72 [SOURCE: Page_73]

2016). Because sowing density influences within-crop competition for light, nutrients, and water, it was tested as an adaptation strategy for common bean in Mexico (Baez-Gonzalez et al., 2020) and for soybean in Brazil (Battisti et al., 2018). Increasing crop density led to higher yields due to faster leaf area development (resulting in better radiation interception) and reduced soil evaporation (Battisti et al., 2018). However, increasing sowing density for common bean also led to a higher occurrence of water stress (Baez-Gonzalez et al., 2020). [SOURCE: Page_74]

### 3.2.4. Irrigation

A positive effect of irrigation was found in a majority of papers (92%), with yield increases ranging from 8% to more than 100% (Figure 2.8). In Germany, both common bean (Wagner et al., 2016) and field pea yields (Nendel et al., 2014) have been shown to increase with irrigation. Yield gains were also identified for soybean in eastern Europe (Elliott et al., 2014; Marković et al., 2020; Mihailović et al., 2015), in Iran (Araji et al., 2018), in the USA (Bao et al., 2015b; Ma et al., 2021; Timilsina et al., 2023; Zhu et al., 2019), and in southern Brazil (Battisti et al., 2018). Conversely, in the USA, Lychuk et al. (2017) and Paul et al. (2020) found a non- significant or negative impact of irrigation on soybean yields in the long term, possibly due to higher nutrient leaching under irrigated conditions. Irrigation was found to reduce yield instability for American soybean by Zhu et al. (2019). [SOURCE: Page_74]

Although irrigation seemed to be an effective strategy for adapting grain legume cultivation to climate change, authors’ hypotheses that were incorporated into simulations did not always reflect the reality experienced by farmers. In particular, irrigation water availability was often considered infinite, whereas huge challenges already exist today in water availability. Future water availability was assessed in only two papers, with diverging results. Wagner et al. (2016) estimated that only 33% to 43% of the Saxony state in Germany could provide all the water required for field pea irrigation under future climate conditions. In contrast, Elliott et al. (2014) estimated that renewable water available for irrigation would still exceed demand in most European countries. [SOURCE: Page_74]

The uncertainty of future water availability implied by these studies strongly suggests that optimizing irrigation systems will be required to make the most of the available water resources. Irrigation system optimization was studied by Amiri et al. (2021) who pointed out the importance of irrigation timing. They found that chickpea benefitted more from supplemental irrigation at the pod-filling stage than at the flowering stage. Sub-irrigation (i.e. water capture and recycling for summer irrigation) was another optimization strategy that was studied by Baule et al. (2017). [SOURCE: Page_74]

### 3.2.5. Soil management

Soil management options were presented as interesting strategies for designing “climate-smart” systems that incorporate both adaptation and mitigation. Climate change is expected to affect N processes (e.g., enhanced mineralization, reduced biological nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_74]

73 [SOURCE: Page_74]

fixation) and thus indirectly impact crop performances (Elli et al., 2022; Malone et al., 2020). Management options such as reduced tillage, crop residue incorporation, and cover crops all play a role in N processes. These options were therefore assessed both for the environmental services they could provide and for their potential to sustain crop performances in the context of climate change. [SOURCE: Page_75]

Switching to conservation tillage or implementing a cover crop during the previous winter were two strategies tested for soybean (Figure 2.7). These two management options were found to have a positive impact on water storage (Li et al., 2021), soil erosion, and nutrient leaching (Panagopoulos et al., 2014). The effect of reduced tillage on crop performances was found neutral (He et al., 2018; Panagopoulos et al., 2014, 2015; Parajuli et al., 2016), except in He et al. (2018) where zero-tillage reduced yields under severe climate change. Likewise, implementing a rye or a wheat cover crop in winter had no significant effect in Li et al. (2021), Malone et al. (2020), and Panagopoulos et al. (2015). A slight yield reduction was observed in Basche et al. (2016) and Panagopoulos et al. (2014), especially for dry years, and was attributed to the competition between soybean and the cover crop for nutrients and soil water. As these two options provided environmental services without significantly harming crop yields, they were advocated for use as mitigation and adaptation strategies (Malone et al., 2020). [SOURCE: Page_75]

Crop residue incorporation was assessed in several papers in Australia to reduce soil erosion and improve water and organic matter content. Neither chickpea (Liu et al., 2017) nor narrow-leaved lupin (Wang et al., 2019) nor field pea (He et al., 2022) responded to residue management. Adapting fertilization to increase crop yields under climate change was also found to be inefficient (Lychuk et al., 2017; Wang et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_75]

### 4. Discussion

---

[CHUNK_METADATA]

- Parent_Topic: 5226380

- Summary: Section focusing on 4.1.        uncertainty of climate change’s impact on future grain legume production in europe

- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation Research

### 4.1.        Uncertainty of climate change’s impact on future grain legume production in Europe

Conclusions on the impact of climate change mostly apply to soybean, as very few data were available for the other crops. For soybean, we found a good agreement between studies, with yield gains found in northern Europe and a higher probability of yield losses in southern and south-eastern Europe. This spatial pattern of climate change impact is consistent with expectations from climate projections and crop physiology knowledge. Indeed, while climate change is expected to lengthen crop growing seasons in the north, it may lead to faster crop development in the south and increase the occurrence of stresses, eventually causing yield losses (Osborne et al., 2013). A similar spatial pattern was found for wheat, another C$_3$ crop, by Hristov et al. (2020). [SOURCE: Page_75]

These conclusions were relatively robust across climate scenarios, time periods, and types of models. In some regions, the divergences between studies may be explained by differences [SOURCE: Page_75]

74 [SOURCE: Page_75]

in model inputs (e.g., climate models and scenarios), structure, and parameters (Wallach and Thorburn, 2017). Additional work is needed to quantify these sources of uncertainty, as has been done for major crops (Li et al., 2015; Rosenzweig et al., 2014). In particular, a major source of uncertainty arises from models that often neglect the impact of CO$_2$ (considered in only 36% of selected papers), ozone (4%), and abiotic stresses such as cold snap and waterlogging (not assessed in this review). [SOURCE: Page_76]

Our analysis could not assess whether yield gains in the north will compensate for yield losses in current soybean production hotspots, which are mainly located in southern and eastern Europe (Appendix A, Figure A.2). The few results that are available presented diverging conclusions. For example, Guilpart et al. (2022) showed a reduction of high-yielding production areas under climate change. In contrast, Nendel et al. (2023) found an overall productivity gain with the same scenarios and crop. To assess how climate change will impact the average grain legume yield and production at the European scale and identify future production hotspots, we suggest that future simulations should provide absolute yield values instead of relative values, since relative changes can easily reach very high values when the baseline yield is low. Thus, since grain legume yields are currently very low in northern Europe, even a strong increase due to climate change may not be enough to reach attractive yields for farmers. [SOURCE: Page_76]

---

### 4.2.        Adaptation strategies for mitigating the impact of climate change on grain legumes

This review shows that adaptation strategies have the potential to mitigate the negative impacts of climate change. However, the strategies investigated differed in their effects on yields. Irrigation and crop management options resulted in significant yield gains overall. In contrast, a neutral or slightly negative effect on yields was frequently simulated for alternative soil management and fertilization, which was not surprising given grain legume reliance on biological nitrogen fixation (Liu et al., 2017). Nevertheless, these options provided secondary benefits, for example soil erosion prevention, which could motivate their adoption by European farmers. [SOURCE: Page_76]

Our analysis reveals an imbalance between relatively well-studied strategies (e.g., irrigation or change in sowing dates) and strategies whose potential has yet to be examined (e.g., intercropping). Notably, combining different adaptation strategies appears promising but has not yet been studied. In particular, we suggest that water-saving soil management options should be tested in combination with optimized irrigation systems. This combination of strategies would sustain yields in spite of the growing pressure on water resources. Engaging stakeholders in the co-design of adaptation strategies would help identify relevant combinations and increase the scope of strategies tested (Farrell et al., 2023; Tui et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_76]

Our assessment of the adaptation effect involves some limitations both from the method used in the review and in selected papers. First, our work does not consider the dynamic [SOURCE: Page_76]

75 [SOURCE: Page_76]

evolution of adaptation efficiency. Lobell (2014) distinguishes between an “impact-neutral” adaptation strategy (e.g., a strategy enhancing yields by 10% both in baseline and future), and an “impact-reducing” strategy (e.g., a strategy enhancing yields by 10% in baseline climate and 20% in the future, and thus mitigating the impact of climate change on yields). Impact-reducing strategies are likely to be the key to resilient and sustainable systems and should therefore receive more attention than strategies whose efficiency decreases with time. However, in this review, the effect of adaptation was quantified as the ratio between yield with and without adaptation in the future, which did not allow us to identify impact-reducing strategies. Our choice was mainly dictated by data availability, as only 36% of selected papers provided a complete dataset that include yields with and without adaptation for baseline climate. The few results available sometimes diverged (Appendix A, Figure A.12). In particular, irrigated soybean benefitted less from climate change than rainfed soybean in several studies (Bao et al., 2015b; Ma et al., 2021; Nendel et al., 2023), while contradictory results were found in others (Marković et al., 2020; Timilsina et al., 2023). This issue needs further investigation in order to avoid overestimating adaptation benefits from some strategies. [SOURCE: Page_77]

Second, the effect of adaptation was generally assessed independently of the technical and economic feasibility of the strategy considered. In particular, 90% of studies assessing the effect of irrigation did not estimate future water availability. It is also uncertain whether virtual genotypes obtained by varying crop traits could realistically be developed in breeding programs. In addition, a significant number of studies overlooked the cost of adaptation. For strategies such as irrigation and increased sowing density, this cost may outweigh the increase in crop productivity (Elliott et al., 2014). The evaluation of adaptation strategies would be improved by the development of multi-criteria assessment methodologies including stakeholders’ constraints and objectives (Naulleau et al., 2021, 2022). [SOURCE: Page_77]

### 4.3.       Knowledge gaps and future research avenues

---

[CHUNK_METADATA]

- Parent_Topic: 5226380

- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation Research

- Confidence_Score: High

### 4.3.1. Grain legumes other than soybean as blind spots

Our review highlighted a need for further European-scale modelling, especially for grain legumes other than soybean. Similarly to Magrini et al. (2019), we found a great imbalance between soybean and other grain legumes in the literature, as soybean represented approximately 80% of selected studies. The paucity of data for non-soybean legume crops contrasts with their prominent positions in European environmental, agricultural, and nutritional policies. We recommend prioritizing research on key species including field pea, faba bean, lentil, and chickpea. To fully comprehend the impact of climate change on these crops, it is important to differentiate between spring and winter-sown cultivars, as well as rainfed and irrigated crops. However, this differentiation was not consistently made in the papers selected for this review. [SOURCE: Page_77]

76 [SOURCE: Page_77]

Likewise, simulations at the European scale were scarce. Most data on climate change impact originated from global-scale simulations whose coarse resolution masks a wide local heterogeneity (Zhao et al., 2015). Enlarging the study area to climatically similar regions allowed us to compensate for the lack of data in Europe. However, results transferability may be limited by differences in non-climatic parameters such as soil and farm characteristics. Additional work at the European scale is necessary to support the development of grain legumes, including modelling and data collection for model calibration and evaluation. [SOURCE: Page_78]

---

[CHUNK_METADATA]

- Parent_Topic: 5226380

- Summary: Section focusing on 4.3.2. choice of the right time and spatial scales

- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation Research

- Confidence_Score: High

---

### 4.3.2. Choice of the right time and spatial scales

The number of studies assessing the impact of climate change on soybean in the second half of the XXIst century contrasts with the lack of results for the first half. Identifying the timing of risks and key adaptations is necessary to ensure that time periods used in simulations are not disconnected from stakeholders’ needs (Challinor et al., 2018). We therefore suggest that farmers and other stakeholders should be included in the process of modelling in order to identify relevant inputs and outputs for future simulations (Naulleau et al., 2022). Stakeholders may also guide an adequate choice of a baseline period to avoid misestimating the impact of climate change. [SOURCE: Page_78]

Climate change modelling researchers should also reflect on the choice of the spatial scale used in simulations. The effect of some adaptation strategies (e.g., sowing date and cultivar choice) was found to be site-specific, which highlights the need for a local design of adaptation strategies. For other strategies (e.g., shifting to species originating from other agricultural regions, switching grain legume species), effect assessment may be more relevant at the European scale. [SOURCE: Page_78]

The appropriate choice of time and spatial scale will almost certainly depend on the needs of specific stakeholders. To meet the needs of all stakeholders will likely require multi- scale modelling, as advocated by Peng et al. (2020), who designed a framework from the gene to global scale. In the case of grain legumes, such a framework should also include crop sequence modelling, since benefits from legumes are strongly dependent on their break-crop effects. Future analyses should also account for the response of cropping mix to climate change. Indeed, the impact of climate change and the effect of adaptation are crop-specific and may lead to changes in crop relative performances and economic profitability. To our knowledge, no study has compared grain legume and cereal response to climate change and adaptation. Further research should explore this issue to investigate whether climate change could enhance or reduce grain legume attractiveness to farmers compared to major crops or innovative minor candidate crops. [SOURCE: Page_78]

---

### 4.3.3. A need to broaden the scope of estimated climate change impact

The papers reviewed in our analysis mainly quantified the impact of climate change and the effect of adaptation on grain legume yields. However, yield is only one component of the [SOURCE: Page_78]

77 [SOURCE: Page_78]

lock-in hindering the development of grain legumes in Europe (Magrini et al., 2016). Other indicators of interest for the stakeholders were poorly investigated, in particular the impact of climate change on biotic stresses, yield stability, and services provided by grain legumes. [SOURCE: Page_79]

Biotic stresses (weeds, pests, and pathogens) can lead to significant yield losses (Savary et al., 2019) and are likely to be impacted by climate change. Indeed, with the global rise of temperatures, some insects could establish themselves in areas where their proliferation is currently limited by cold temperatures (Bebber et al., 2013; Chaloner et al., 2021). Rising temperatures could also lead to a shortening of reproductive cycles and an increased number of pest generations during crop growing season, thus increasing the influence of pests on yield losses. Elevated CO$_2$ could increase the relative competitiveness of C$_3$ weeds and make crops less nutritious for insect pests, leading to increased damage (Olesen and Bindi, 2002). In our review, few papers discussed the expected biotic pressure under climate change in Europe, even though this pressure will likely be a concern in the future. Most studies used suitability indexes to assess the future overlap between areas suitable to crops and pests, but yield losses were not estimated. Models simulating plant growth and insect life cycles have already been used in other regions of the world to predict future insect damage (Taylor et al., 2018) and could provide valuable information on potential biotic risks and adaptation strategies in Europe. [SOURCE: Page_79]

Farming systems are particularly sensitive to yield shocks (Hristov et al., 2020), and even systems well-adapted to long-term trends will not necessarily be the most resilient against extreme climate events (Rosenzweig and Tubiello, 2007). We therefore recommend that projections of future yield variability or risks of crop failure should complement existing data on future average yields. Assessing the impact of climate variability on yields will require improving model calibration and representation of climate extremes and CO$_2$ effect, especially for minor crops such as grain legumes (Kersebaum, 2022; Rötter et al., 2018). Designing successful strategies to cope with climate variability may also require new approaches. For example, instead of relying on future climate estimates, adaptation strategies could be designed with the aim of being effective under a wide range of climate conditions (Corbeels et al., 2018). [SOURCE: Page_79]

Finally, as grain legumes are often grown for the N-related ecosystem services they provide, we suggest that future simulations should investigate the impact of climate change on N fixation and provision. Non-yield benefits of adaptation strategies should also be assessed, as well as potential interactions between adaptation and climate change mitigation. Given Europe’s growing interest in grain legumes as high-quality protein crops, changes in grain quality and protein content should be studied to complement already existing experimental data (Scheelbeek et al., 2018). [SOURCE: Page_79]

78 [SOURCE: Page_79]

5. Conclusion This systematic review provides an original synthesis of model-based studies simulating the impact of climate change and the effect of adaptation on grain legume performances in Europe. Overall, the positive impact of climate change on soybean yields in northern Europe was relatively consistent among studies, while yield losses may be expected in southern and eastern Europe. Although the spatial pattern appeared similar to soybean, lack of data prevented drawing a robust conclusion for other grain legumes at the European scale. Irrigation, changes in sowing date, and cultivar choice were among the most promising adaptation strategies, although authors seldom assessed their environmental desirability and economic feasibility. Alternative soil management generally had a neutral or negative impact on yields but provided secondary benefits that could motivate its adoption by farmers. [SOURCE: Page_80]

The main knowledge gaps identified were a lack of data for non-soybean grain legumes and a need for more Europe-focused studies, especially for adaptation effect assessment. Modelling the impact of climate change and adaptation remains an open research avenue for key crops such as field pea and faba bean. Only a few studies considered crop response to elevated CO$_2$, ozone, and biotic pressure, and incorporating these factors would enhance climate change impact assessment. We also suggest that future simulations should broaden the range of adaptation tested (e.g., intercropping, choice of the grain legume species, combinations of several adaptation strategies) and indicators assessed (e.g., economic indicators, yield variability), in the frame of a multi-criteria analysis. [SOURCE: Page_80]

These points highlight a research focus on just a few aspects of climate change and adaptation, leaving in the dark important issues and challenges for stakeholders. Involving stakeholders would help orient future modelling, in order to provide relevant outputs to inform adaptation, within the scope of a use-oriented approach. [SOURCE: Page_80]

79 [SOURCE: Page_80]

Chapitre 3 : Diagnostiquer les défis liés au changement climatique et à l’adaptation auprès des acteurs des filières [SOURCE: Page_81]

Marie Marteau-Bazouni, Marie-Hélène Jeuffroy, Nicolas Guilpart [SOURCE: Page_81]

Ce chapitre a été soumis sous le titre Stakeholders’ perceptions of climate change point to future challenges for the adaptation of grain legumes à Agronomy for Sustainable Development (novembre 2024). [SOURCE: Page_81]

Abstract The diversification of cropping systems using grain legumes has been identified as a crucial lever in the transition towards more sustainable agriculture in Europe. However, climate change will likely impact the yields and services provided by these crops. Increasing the production area of grain legumes will therefore require the design of effective adaptation strategies. To develop such tailored adaptation strategies, it is essential to consider the needs of the various stakeholders involved in the future development of grain legume value chains, and therefore to examine their perceptions and activities. This study evidences stakeholders’ perceptions of climate change and its impacts on grain legumes, characterizes the diversity of adaptation options they envision, and identifies barriers to those adaptations. We conducted interviews with stakeholders involved at various stages of the value chains for five major grain legume species in France. Stakeholders’ perceptions depended on their location and the grain legume species considered. Although most respondents perceived climate change as a constraint for grain legumes, they identified opportunities for specific crops and regions. The stakeholders interviewed had either observed, imagined, or experimented with a diverse range of adaptation options. Despite this diversity, the respondents reported barriers of various natures that need to be removed to support adaptation. This diagnosis points to three major [SOURCE: Page_81]

80 [SOURCE: Page_81]

challenges for the adaptation of grain legumes: (i) addressing the antagonistic symptoms of climate change, the perceived increase in climate variability, and the lack of methods for anticipating long-term climatic changes; (ii) dealing simultaneously with the adaptation to climate change and the unlocking of socio-technical systems regarding grain legumes; and (iii) filling knowledge gaps on grain legume response to climate change. To our knowledge, this is the first study to probe stakeholders’ perceptions of climate change and identify opportunities and challenges for the adaptation of grain legumes in Europe. [SOURCE: Page_82]

Keywords: Grain legumes; field pea; faba bean; soybean; lentil; chickpea; adaptation; climate change; survey; France [SOURCE: Page_82]

1. Introduction Diversifying cropping systems with grain legumes is often proposed as a powerful means to contribute to the transition towards more sustainable agriculture in Europe (Köpke and Nemecek, 2010; Peoples et al., 2019; Watson et al., 2017). Their ability to fix atmospheric nitrogen (N) means grain legumes provide numerous environmental and agronomic services, such as reduced greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions from synthetic fertilizer production and application, increased soil nitrogen availability, and pest and disease control. Grain legumes also supply high-protein grains that can partly replace animal-based proteins to reduce the environmental footprint of European diets (Davis et al., 2010). Several studies have demonstrated the efficiency of an increased grain legume production and consumption to reduce greenhouse gas emissions. Estimations of the GHG abatement from an increased production area of legumes in France range from 0.9 Mt CO2eq.yr-1 (Pellerin et al., 2017) to 2.5 Mt CO2eq.yr-1 (Dequiedt and Moran, 2015), which approximately corresponds to 1-3% of the country’s GHG emissions from agriculture (Citepa, 2024). A diet shift with a partial substitution of proteins from animal products by grain legumes throughout Europe would make a significant dent in global emissions by 2050, with 231 to 259 MtCO2eq of emissions avoided annually (Prudhomme et al., 2020), or approximately 2% of global GHG emissions from agriculture (IPCC, 2023b). The diversification of cropping systems using grain legumes can therefore play a crucial role in mitigating climate change. [SOURCE: Page_82]

Despite the numerous services provided by grain legumes, these crops seldom feature in European cropping systems (Magrini et al., 2016). This is the result of the co-evolution of several factors which have gradually locked in the agri-food sector’s preference for cereals, hindering crop diversification and grain legume development in Europe. This socio-technical lock-in around major crops has been described extensively (Balázs et al., 2021a; Magrini et al., 2016; Meynard et al., 2018; Voisin et al., 2014). It includes such barriers as lower investments in breeding compared with major species; a lack of solutions for pest, disease, and weed control; a lack of knowledge and technical references among farmers and their advisors; and logistical constraints for collectors. Recently, growing concerns about the environmental impact of specialized agriculture have led to a push for the reintroduction of legumes into cropping [SOURCE: Page_82]

81 [SOURCE: Page_82]

systems. To contribute to unlocking the situation and benefit from the many services provided by legumes, policies at European level (such as CAP subsidies) and at national level (such as the French national plant-based protein strategy – MAA, 2020) are incentivizing increases in the production and consumption of grain legumes. [SOURCE: Page_83]

Climate change is likely to have an impact on the yields of grain legumes and the services they provide, which may further hamper their development. Grain legumes are sensitive to numerous biotic and abiotic stresses whose occurrence is likely to increase with climate change (Ahmed et al., 2022). These stresses can reduce yields (Farooq et al., 2017; Gogoi et al., 2018) and increase yield instability (Falconnier et al., 2020), a factor that has already been identified as a barrier to the adoption of grain legumes by farmers (Cernay et al., 2015). Stresses can also reduce the nitrogen services provided by legumes, either directly by reducing symbiotic nitrogen fixation (Serraj et al., 1999) or indirectly by reducing yields, on which crop N fixation partly depends (Herridge et al., 2022). Increasing the area grown with grain legumes will therefore require the implementation of adaptation strategies to increase the resilience of these crops to climate change. [SOURCE: Page_83]

Adaptation in the agricultural sector encompasses a wide range of forms (Howden et al., 2007; Kates et al., 2012; Smit and Skinner, 2002). In this article, we use the definition proposed by Moser and Ekstrom (2010): “Adaptation involves changes in social-ecological systems in response to actual and expected impacts of climate change in the context of interacting non- climatic changes. Adaptation strategies and actions can range from short-term coping to longer- term, deeper transformations, aim to meet more than climate change goals alone, and may or may not succeed in moderating harm or exploiting beneficial opportunities.” This broad definition includes various adaptation strategies at different space and time scales and includes positive and negative interactions with non-climatic factors. [SOURCE: Page_83]

To design relevant innovations for adaptation, it is essential to consider stakeholders’ needs by analyzing their activities (Cerf et al., 2012). In the case of adaptation to climate change, it is crucial to investigate stakeholder’s perceptions of climate change to design effective adaptation strategies (Masud et al., 2017). Several studies have surveyed producers’ and experts’ perceptions of climate change and adaptation in Europe (Battaglini et al., 2009; Morel and Cartau, 2023; Peltonen-Sainio et al., 2020; Woods et al., 2017; Zhao et al., 2022a). They show that farmers have observed climate changes that affect their farming systems and have implemented or devised solutions to adapt to these changes. Some studies analyze farmers’ drivers (Woods et al., 2017) and multifactorial decision-making processes (Morel and Cartau, 2023) to guide policy making. Others show that farmers’ attitudes to adaptation vary according to geographical differences (Battaglini et al., 2009; Peltonen-Sainio et al., 2020; Zhao et al., 2022a) and farm characteristics (Morel and Cartau, 2023; Peltonen-Sainio et al., 2020). Their findings highlight the need for tailored innovations and policy instruments. [SOURCE: Page_83]

82 [SOURCE: Page_83]

Research on adaptation to climate change has however focused mostly on cereal production and viticulture, whereas grain legumes have often been studied as mitigation solutions (e.g., Ferreira et al, 2021; Schneider and Huyghe, 2015). Consequently, few data are available on grain legume response to climate change and adaptation options (Marteau- Bazouni et al., 2024). This study helps to fill this knowledge gap by answering the following research questions: (i) how do stakeholders involved in the development of grain legumes perceive the evolution of climate and its impacts on these crops, (ii) how do they (or other stakeholders) plan to adapt, and (iii) what are the barriers impeding the process of adaptation? [SOURCE: Page_84]

To answer these questions, we conducted a survey in France inspired by the diagnosis of uses method (Cerf et al., 2012; Clerino et al., 2023; Ravier et al., 2016). Our study includes five major grain legume species, namely soybean (Glycine max (L.) Merr.), field pea (Pisum sativum L.), faba bean (Vicia faba L.), lentil (Lens culinaris Medik.), and chickpea (Cicer arietinum L.). These five species represented 98% of the grain legume production area in France in 2020 (SAA, 2020). We conducted interviews with stakeholders at various stages of the value chains to understand their perception of climate change, characterize the diversity of adaptation options and identify barriers to the process of adaptation. To our knowledge, this is the first study to offer a detailed analysis of stakeholders’ views on the impact of climate change on grain legumes in a European country and the opportunities for and challenges to their adaptation. [SOURCE: Page_84]

### 2.1.        Survey design

We conducted a total of 33 semi-structured interviews with 42 stakeholders. Because of the systemic nature of the lock-in hampering grain legume development, we interviewed stakeholders involved at different stages of the value chains, from seed breeding to grain processing. We feared that interviewing farmers directly might yield a scattered body of overly specific answers, so we chose to rely instead on the input of other stakeholders working at broader scales. [SOURCE: Page_85]

Interviewees were identified through snowball sampling. First, we selected advisors from ten regional Chambers of Agriculture (local extension services) to cover a large part of the French territory. Interviewees were then asked to recommend people in other professional sectors. Eventually, we conducted interviews with professionals from cooperatives (10 interviews), local extension services (10), national extension services (6), seed breeding (4), seed multiplication (1), food and feed industry (1) and French administration (1). [SOURCE: Page_85]

Interviewees were located across the country. Their location was defined as either their geographical area of expertise (for extension services) or their supply area (for cooperatives and industries). Fifteen interviewees were located in the north-west (Brittany, Normandy, Pays de la Loire, and Centre-Val de Loire), ten in the north-east (Hauts-de-France, Grand Est, and Bourgogne-Franche-Comté), and nine in the south (Nouvelle-Aquitaine, Occitanie, Auvergne- Rhône-Alpes, and Provence-Alpes-Côte d’Azur). The locations of four (resp. one) interviewees straddled two (resp. three) zones; their answers are therefore counted in more than one zone (Figure 3.2). In addition, five interviewees provided expertise at national level. [SOURCE: Page_85]

84 [SOURCE: Page_85]

Figure 3.2: Interviewees' locations and professions. Interviewees’ location is determined by either their area of expertise (for extension services) or their supply basin (for cooperatives and industries). The number of interviews in each zone (south, north-west, and north-east) is indicated in parentheses. Five interviewees provided expertise at the country level and are labeled “FR”. Note that the locations of four (resp. one) interviewees straddled two (resp. three) zones; their answers are therefore counted in more than one zone. [SOURCE: Page_86]

The interviews were conducted remotely, either by phone (3 interviews) or video conferencing (30). Each interview lasted on average 60 minutes (minimum 35 – maximum 120 minutes) and addressed the following topics: [SOURCE: Page_86]

-   How do your professional activities contribute to the development of grain legumes? -   Do you think climate change is a barrier or an opportunity to the development of grain legumes in your region? How has climate evolved according to you and how has this impacted grain legumes? -   How do you (or other stakeholders) plan on adapting your (or their) activities to develop grain legumes in the context of climate change? -   Which information on climate change do you use in your professional activities to help you adapt to it? Which information would you need? At which space and time scales? [SOURCE: Page_86]

Interviews were not limited to these topics, however. Interviewees could also mention other topics they considered relevant. [SOURCE: Page_86]

85 [SOURCE: Page_86]

### 2.2.        Interview data analysis

Twenty-seven interviews were recorded and transcribed, either manually (6 interviews) or using the free software NoScribe (github.com/kaixxx/noScribe) (21 interviews). For the six interviews that could not be recorded, detailed notes were taken. [SOURCE: Page_87]

The interview transcripts were coded manually using the free software Taguette (www.taguette.org) to identify and categorize (i) the impacts of climate change on grain legumes, (ii) adaptation strategies, and (iii) barriers to adaptation mentioned by the interviewees. By clustering similar responses, we obtained a coding pattern that emerge from the body of interviews. We labeled propositions “adaptation options” when they were either (i) currently being experimented with or implemented by the interviewees themselves; or (ii) currently being experimented with or implemented by farmers and observed by the interviewees; or (iii) devised by the interviewees but yet to be experimented with either by themselves or by farmers. Following Moser and Ekstrom (2010), we applied the “barriers to adaptation” label to obstacles that make adaptation less efficient or more costly and can be overcome with innovative thinking and concerted effort. [SOURCE: Page_87]

### 3.1.1. Relations between stakeholders’ perceptions, location, and grain legume species

Stakeholders’ perceptions of climate change were complex, with almost all interviewees (96%) outlining two or more concurrent changes impacting grain legumes. On average, 4.5 concomitant aspects of climate change were mentioned in each interview. [SOURCE: Page_87]

An overwhelming majority of our interviewees – 97% – expressed concerns about climate change’s negative impacts on grain legumes, while only 33% of them saw it as an opportunity (Figure 3.3). Stakeholders’ views on climate change depended on the species considered. They mostly mentioned negative impacts of climate change on field pea and faba bean, while they identified both negative and positive impacts it could have on lentil, chickpea, and soybean. Stakeholders’ views on climate change and its impacts also differed depending on their location (Figure 3.4). Some issues were mentioned more frequently in the south, such as the increasing biotic pressure and uncertain water availability, while others were mentioned more frequently in the north, like the increasing frequency of heavy rains. [SOURCE: Page_87]

86 [SOURCE: Page_87]

Figure 3.3: Positive and negative impacts of climate change mentioned in the interviews depending on the grain legume species considered. The results are presented as a percentage of the total number of interviews (n=33). Although we tried to differentiate between spring and winter crops, this was not possible in every interview. Therefore, the column labeled “field pea” (resp. “faba bean”) includes, but is not strictly equal to, the sum of the results for spring and winter field pea (resp. spring and winter faba bean). [SOURCE: Page_88]

Figure 3.4: Positive and negative impacts of climate change mentioned in the interviews depending on the interviewees’ geographical area of expertise. The results are presented for three zones (south, north-east, and north-west) as a percentage of the number of interviews in each zone (indicated in parentheses). Note that the locations of four (resp. one) interviewees straddled two (resp. three) zones; their answers are therefore counted in more than one zone. [SOURCE: Page_88]

87 [SOURCE: Page_88]

### 3.1.2. A wide range of climatic constraints impacting grain legumes

The interviewees described multiple negative impacts of climate change on grain legumes. Respondents from all three zones (Figure 3.4) reported an ongoing increase in the frequency of droughts (mentioned in 79% of the interviews) and heat stresses (76%). Specifically, they highlighted an increase in the frequency of these stresses during reproductive stages, causing flower abortion, reduced grain filling, and eventually yield losses. In addition, some interviewees (27%) reported an increasing uncertainty about the timing of droughts and heat stresses. They described stresses occurring earlier in the growing season or at unexpected times, resulting in reduced biomass and symbiotic fixation: “The problem is that heat waves can now crop up over a single week. We are seeing more and more of these spikes, and they are increasingly intense. They can occur during sowing or flowering, or when crops have reached maturity” (I$_3$). [SOURCE: Page_89]

An increase in biotic pressure was mentioned in 58% of the interviews and an increase in pest pressure during the spring in 42% of the interviews, especially in the south and the north- west. Respondents described earlier and more intense pest attacks from pea aphids Acyrthospihon pisum (Harris) and weevils (Bruchus spp. and Sitona spp.) on lentil, field pea, and faba bean. They attributed this to milder winters and warmer springs accelerating pest life cycles. They also reported the spread of new insect pests in regions where they were not historically a problem (18% of the interviews), including infestations of Bruchus in the north of France and pod borers Etiella zinckenella (Treitschke) in the south. Similarly, an increasing pathogen pressure, which interviewees attributed to milder winters and an increased risk of damage from late frost, was reported in 36% of the interviews, especially on winter field pea and winter faba bean in the north. Some interviewees (9%) also reported the emergence of new pathogens in the north (complex of Ascochyta sp., Colletotrichum sp., and Pseudomonas syringae), linked to milder winters and springs. [SOURCE: Page_89]

Symptoms of climate change also included increasing climatic variability. This was reported in 58% of the interviews, especially in the south and north-east. Interviewees described it as a lack of seasonality, erratic or unprecedented weather events, and increasing year-on-year unpredictability: “Warming is a part [of climate change], but there are also climate hazards. When it rains, we get considerable amounts of water, when it’s hot, it’s often very hot, but, conversely, we are getting some very dry spells… and climates that are out of sync – or at least more random – and not as clear-cut as they used to be” (I13). It was perceived both as an ongoing trend (“Over the last 15-20 years […] it used to be once every now and then, and now it’s every second or third year. So now every year is atypical, but one time it’ll be frost, the other it’ll be floods…” I$_7$) and a challenge for the future (“Today, we try to think forward, but it’s not easy, because we don’t know what tomorrow will bring” I12). Specifically, a third (33%) of the interviewees reported that the increasing climatic variability hindered their ability to learn from one year to the next and to design adaptation strategies. [SOURCE: Page_89]

88 [SOURCE: Page_89]

The interviewees reported an increasing temperature range in winter and early spring (mentioned in 45% of the interviews in all three zones). They described this as an increasing contrast between milder mean temperatures and sudden cold waves occurring during the winter (mentioned in 15% of the interviews) or the spring (30%). According to them, these cold waves were less gradual, more severe, or occurred later than they historically had: “Though mean temperatures have increased, these cold dips are more severe. The temperature can be around 5-10°C, and then two days later dip to a low of around -10°C” (I$_3$). “It used to be less problematic because we didn’t get -5°C or -10°C in early April” (I16). Interviewees reported an increasing risk of mechanical damage and pest and pathogen outbreaks due to this broadening temperature range, especially for winter field pea and winter faba bean. [SOURCE: Page_90]

A more uneven distribution of precipitation and an increasing frequency of heavy rains were mentioned in 42% of the interviews, especially in the north-east. Some interviewees (21%) reported an increasing frequency of heavy rains and storms in summer, resulting in crop lodging. They also perceived a rise in precipitation during winter (15%), resulting in restricted time windows for tillage operations and sowing. Finally, uncertain access to irrigation due to an increasing competition for water resources was reported in 39% of the interviews, especially for soybean in the south. [SOURCE: Page_90]

- Confidence_Score: High

### 3.1.3. A few opportunities for some species and regions

The interviewees reported two main positive impacts of climate change on grain legumes. One-third (30%) mentioned warmer and drier climates as an opportunity to grow lentil, chickpea, and soybean, especially in the north. According to them, the increasing mean temperature allows these species to meet their thermal requirements until harvest in new areas. For example, they identified an opportunity for soybean in the north and Nouvelle-Aquitaine (south). In Brittany (north-west), drier springs were also seen as an opportunity for winter crops due to reduced disease pressure. Finally, drier and warmer climates would provide a competitive advantage to drought- and heat-tolerant species such as chickpea compared with other species. 21% of respondents told us that lentil, chickpea, and soybean were already expanding northward (“species such as soybean, chickpea, lentil, are moving northwards, actually. Growing them is increasingly feasible” I20). Others (9%) described it as an opportunity for the future (“I think that chickpea has a part to play in the years to come and that it’s a crop that could remain in France in the long run”, I27). [SOURCE: Page_90]

A few interviewees (9%) noticed a reduction in frost days in winter and early spring, allowing for an extension of sowing windows. Their perception contrasted with the increasing risk of frost damage mentioned by other interviewees (45%). This contradiction could not be explained by either the interviewees’ location or the crop species considered. [SOURCE: Page_90]

89 [SOURCE: Page_90]

### 3.2.       Adaptation options to increase grain legume production despite climate change

---

[CHUNK_METADATA]

- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation Research

---

### 3.2.1. A variety of adaptation options identified by the interviewees

In the interviews, we identified a diverse range of adaptation strategies that interviewees had already implemented, observed or at least devised (Figure 3.5). We identified six main adaptation levers, namely: (i) changing the cultivated varieties (mentioned in 82% of the interviews); (ii) adapting crop management (82%); (iii) redesigning cropping systems that include grain legumes (39%); (iv) shifting crop production areas (88%); (v) securing producers’ and stakeholders’ income (76%); and (vi) producing and circulating knowledge (61%). These levers differ in their scopes (ranging from the scale of the plant to that of production areas, including the cropping system), objectives (from stabilizing grain legume yields to securing stakeholders’ income), and the actors they involve. Each lever is further divided into several options, as described below. [SOURCE: Page_91]

Figure 3.5: Adaptation options extracted from the interviews. Adaptation options are classified into six levers of various scales and scopes. The percentage of interviewees who mentioned each option is indicated in parentheses. [SOURCE: Page_91]

90 [SOURCE: Page_91]

---

[CHUNK_METADATA]

- Summary: Section focusing on 3.2.2. changing the cultivated varieties

- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation Research

- Confidence_Score: High

---

### 3.2.2. Changing the cultivated varieties

The “varieties” lever included two main options. The first option, breeding new cultivars that are tolerant to biotic and abiotic stresses, was mentioned both by breeders and by other stakeholders (cooperatives and extensions services) in 76% of the interviews. According to breeders, breeding mechanically selects for cultivars adapted to climate change: “In selection, we are confronted with climate constraints. So, de facto, we select, even passively, varieties that are more tolerant to these stresses” (I$_1$). Other stakeholders hoped that future breeding would provide new cultivars more tolerant to drought and heat stresses (expressed in 39% of the interviews), diseases (6%), and frost (3%): “Effective genetic selection […] could help us deal with water stress, heat stress, it could help us deal with diseases” (I$_9$). Breeding was described by both the breeders surveyed and other interviewees as a long-term option, as it takes about ten years to breed a new variety. [SOURCE: Page_92]

The second option, proposed as a shorter-term one, was improving cultivar choices. Extension services and cooperatives suggested that already available cultivars provide enough resources to adapt to climate change (24% of the interviews). Some of them have conducted variety trials to optimize cultivar choices (e.g., identifying cultivars that are less sensitive to drought and heat stresses). [SOURCE: Page_92]

### 3.2.3. Adapting crop management

The “crop management” lever included a wide range of options. This lever was mentioned mainly by interviewees from cooperatives and national and local extension services. The various adaptation options mentioned have either been experimented with by farmers, are currently being studied by the interviewees in field trials, or are recommended by the interviewees based on their experience and knowledge of grain legume physiology. These options are part of an ongoing adaptation process to adjust to already perceptible changes: “We have already been adapting […] We are on a short time scale, not necessarily long-term climate change, but it’s necessarily linked with effects from climate modifications.” (I28) [SOURCE: Page_92]

Farmers and extension services have already been experimenting with early sowing to avoid drought and heat stresses during reproductive stages, as reported in 58% of interviews. Conversely, late sowing of winter crops to reduce disease incidence was recommended by 18% of the interviewees. In the south, where winter temperatures are mild, some farmers have tried sowing spring pea or lentil in winter to escape drought and heat stresses, as reported in 6% of the interviews. [SOURCE: Page_92]

Increasing irrigation was mentioned as an adaptation option by 24% of interviewees all over France, yet most of them (18%) deemed this option unrealistic due to the increasing pressure on water resources. Some interviewees (12%) considered studying the optimization of irrigation management and timing to maximize crop water use efficiency. [SOURCE: Page_92]

91 [SOURCE: Page_92]

Reduced tillage was mentioned in 21% of the interviews, either to increase soil water retention (to adapt to increasing drought), or to improve the soil’s load-bearing capacity (to adapt to increasing precipitation in winter), or else to allow for early sowing (to adapt to spring droughts). Reducing sowing density to reduce disease incidence and crop competition for water was either recommended or experimented with by 15% of the interviewees. [SOURCE: Page_93]

Some interviewees (12%, mainly cooperatives) have studied the efficiency of biostimulants to stimulate crop resistance to drought and heat, while 9% plan on studying the optimization of pesticide application. [SOURCE: Page_93]

### 3.2.4. Redesigning cropping systems including grain legumes

In response to the increasing frequency of stresses and its negative impact on grain legume yield stability, designing alternative systems was proposed as a way to reduce the risk associated with growing grain legumes as main crops. 39% of interviewees had either devised or observed this “cropping system” lever. [SOURCE: Page_93]

Intercropping was mentioned in 21% of the interviews and had been explored mainly for lentil, field pea and faba bean. This option was proposed to adapt to heavy rains (e.g., camelina- lentil intercropping to avoid lodging) and increasing biotic pressure, as well as to stabilize income: “If you grow several species on the same plot, even if one species fails, the other allows for some degree of profitability” (I$_1$). [SOURCE: Page_93]

Some interviewees (18%) suggested or observed a diversification of grain legume uses within crop rotations (cover crops, forage crop mixtures): “We can also observe people who consider grain legumes, not necessarily as cash crops, but as double-crops, […], intercrops, cover crops, or as catch crops” (I16). [SOURCE: Page_93]

Another 6% of interviewees, located either in the south or the north-east, had observed soybean double-cropping (i.e. growing it as a second crop after a first cash crop the same year on the same plot) or experimented with it. One respondent considered this option as a mean to render cropping systems more flexible in response to increasing climate variability. [SOURCE: Page_93]

### 3.2.5. Shifting crop production areas

The “production area” lever encompasses shifting species production areas (mentioned in 73% of the interviews) and shifting from spring to winter crops (55%). [SOURCE: Page_93]

Interviewees from local extension services and cooperatives proposed introducing new species such as field pea and faba bean to enlarge or replace “traditional” grain legume collections (site-based approach). The new species mentioned included chickpea, lentil, soybean, and exotic species such as lablab. They were introduced in the north-west and north- east of France either to seize an opportunity (e.g., market opportunities for lentils and chickpeas in the human food sector and climatic conditions that are perceived to become [SOURCE: Page_93]

92 [SOURCE: Page_93]

increasingly favorable for growing these species) or to replace traditional species by stress- tolerant species such as chickpea. Some interviewees contributed to implement this option by coordinating trial networks (21%, mostly extension services), modelling present and future crop suitability (including grain legume species) to optimize crop choice locally (9%, cooperatives), and seeking new outlets (6%, cooperatives). Interviewees from national extension services, industries and administrations also anticipated a shift of production areas with a northward displacement of the area suitable for soybean and Mediterranean crops such as lentil and chickpea (national approach). Although this option was frequently mentioned, 30% of the interviewees deemed it limited by economic factors (e.g., lack of market opportunities) and climatic constraints (e.g., no access to irrigation for soybean, less suitable local climatic conditions than expected, and the increasing frequency of stresses even in the north). [SOURCE: Page_94]

Farmers in the north-west and north-east had already experimented with shifting from spring to winter crops, which flower earlier and can thus avoid summer drought and heat, and interviewees from cooperatives and national extension services spoke of it (55%). However, 36% of interviewees reported only limited benefits from this option, due to winter crops’ sensitivity to the increasing risk of late frosts, early stresses affecting winter crop reproductive stages, and heavy precipitation in winter and spring. [SOURCE: Page_94]

### 3.2.6. Securing stakeholders’ income

During interviews, 55% of respondents mentioned the necessity to structure value chains and create more value around grain legumes. Although this does not exclusively address climate change adaptation, value creation or increased policy support (mentioned in 22% of the interviews) would compensate for grain legumes’ poor performances and yield losses due to biotic and abiotic stresses and thus encourage farmers to adopt them: “One must accept to get lower yields, but try to find ways to create value” (I12). [SOURCE: Page_94]

Interviewees from all professional categories came up with this option. Some of them (27%) had contributed to implementing it in various ways: structuring new value chains for chickpea, lentil, and soybean in the north (12%, mostly local extension services), structuring value chains with new market opportunities for field pea in the food industry (6%, mostly cooperatives and industries), creating value using “Made in France” and environmental labels (12%), and informing producers about market opportunities (3%). [SOURCE: Page_94]

Insurances at the farm or the cooperative level were mentioned as an adaptation strategy in 18% of the interviews. Finally, 12% of respondents suggested diversifying grain legume species and sowing dates at farm level (for farmers) or diversifying sources of supply (for cooperatives and industries) to increase system resilience. [SOURCE: Page_94]

93 [SOURCE: Page_94]

### 3.2.7. Creating and circulating knowledge on climate change and grain legumes

Lastly, some interviewees, mostly from local extension services, highlighted the importance of knowledge production and circulation to increase stakeholders’ ability to cope with climate change. Respectively 39% and 21% of interviewees said they produced and disseminated knowledge on grain legumes and on climate change. For 24% of them, the main goal was to inform producers about improved management practices (informing them about the impact of sowing density on disease incidence, for instance); 15% focused on informing them about new species (e.g., technical and economical references about chickpea in the north-west); 12% emphasized raising their awareness about the impacts of future climate change on their productions; and 3% were mostly concerned with studying the effectiveness of options for adaptation and updating technical references (e.g., updating recommendations about sowing dates to avoid stresses during reproductive stages). 12% of interviewees had developed decision support tools to help farmers plan the evolution of their cropping systems (e.g., crop choice and irrigation management). Finally, some interviewees (6%) called for improved weather forecast services to help stakeholders cope with the increased climate variability. [SOURCE: Page_95]

### 3.3.1. A number of barriers limiting adaptation

The diversity of adaptation options interviewees had identified (and already experimented with) contrasts with the pessimistic outlook some of them expressed regarding the future of grain legumes (18%, mostly cooperatives and extension services): “The problem is, we have some areas now where I don’t see how we’ll be able to keep growing these crops. Considering all the reasons we have mentioned, climate change, the loss of some synthetic active ingredients which is problematic, especially for disease control” (I31). “There is a lack of solutions. Today, we don’t see anything coming that could improve the situation” (I$_7$). [SOURCE: Page_95]

This leads us to conclude that, despite the diversity of adaption options mentioned, these options are not satisfactory to the stakeholders. Our interviewees reported several barriers to the adaptation process, classified in this section as climatic barriers, economic barriers, and knowledge gaps (Table 3.1). Removing these barriers appears to be a prerequisite for facilitating adaptation. [SOURCE: Page_95]

### 3.3.2. Addressing the challenges of climate change adaptation

The interviewees in our study perceived the challenges of adapting to climate change as a complex issue. In this section, we outline three constraints that have arisen from their experience, namely the antagonistic symptoms of climate change, the perceived increase in climate variability, and the lack of methods for anticipating long-term climatic changes. [SOURCE: Page_98]

The numerous and sometimes antagonistic symptoms of climate change were perceived as a constraint for some adaptation options. For example, the increased risk of late frost and diseases prevented modifying the sowing date or switching to winter crops to escape drought and heat stresses at reproductive stages: “It will involve early sowing to escape heat stress at flowering, but sowing earlier means getting exposed to frost” (I16). [SOURCE: Page_98]

Due to the numerous stresses impacting grain legumes, adapting to one specific stress may expose the cropping system to others. For example, switching from spring to winter crops may conflict with the provision of services by spring crops, such as the break-crop effect for weed control (mentioned in 9% of the interviews). Therefore, the complexity of climate change and the systemic nature of adaptation (i.e., adjusting management practices to escape one stress may have an impact on the whole cropping system) could reduce the efficiency of adaptation in some cases. [SOURCE: Page_98]

Some interviewees also expressed their inability to implement efficient adaptation solutions due to the increased climate variability they perceived: “Almost every year is an atypical year, and it is likely to get worse, so we’re at a bit of a loss to offer solutions.” (I25) [SOURCE: Page_98]

Interviewees expressed a need for methods that would enable them to anticipate long- term climatic changes. Specifically, breeders said they needed tools that would accelerate the identification and selection of stress-tolerant cultivars (e.g., experiments under controlled conditions, screening tools): “We sort of have to rely on sheer opportunity, […] we have no guarantee to have [climate hazards] every year. So, we would like to run tests under controlled conditions to reproduce all these abiotic stresses as precisely and rigorously as possible […] to identify stress-tolerant varieties and lines more rapidly” (I$_3$). Experts from national extension services also expressed a need for protocols and methods to manage the climate’s constant evolution: “It raises the question of how to work on these levers. Do I try them in the field? If I try them now, will my results be valid for 3-5 years in the current climate? How should I work?” (I28). [SOURCE: Page_98]

These climatic barriers are not specific to grain legumes. Conflicting timescales have already been identified as a barrier to adaptation in others studies (Biesbroek et al., 2011, 2013). Specifically, these studies describe the conflict between the long-term dynamics of climate change and the short-term dynamics of politics and decision-making. Our results complement these studies by highlighting the conflict between the long-term dynamics of climate change and the short-term methods and tools available to the stakeholders to adapt. Although farmers have mentioned climate variability in surveys conducted on other crops (Battaglini et al., 2009; [SOURCE: Page_98]

97 [SOURCE: Page_98]

Lambert and Eise, 2020; Morel and Cartau, 2023), it was not specifically described as a barrier to adaptation. Our findings illustrate the specific hurdle posed by climate variability and antagonistic effects of climate change in the case of grain legumes. Adaptation planning strategies which deal with uncertainty have been proposed in the agriculture sector (Vermeulen et al., 2013a) as well as in other sectors characterized by longer time scales, such as infrastructure (Hallegatte, 2009), forestry, and public policy (Dessai and Hulme, 2004; Fankhauser, 2017). They include concepts such as robust decision-making, iterative processes, or “no-regret” solutions. How to apply these concepts to the adaptation of annual crops like grain legumes, characterized by shorter time scales, is yet to be determined. Similarly, relevant research methods to support stakeholders’ adaptation process have yet to be designed. [SOURCE: Page_99]

### 3.3.3. Simultaneously dealing with the adaptation to climate change and the unlocking of socio-technical systems

Grain legumes will not be the only group of species affected by climate change (see for example the meta-analysis led by Challinor et al. (2014) on cereal yields under climate change). However, the fact that they are less cultivated may hinder efforts to adapt them to climate change. Based on our interviews and the scientific literature, we were able to analyze the intertwinement of climate change with the socio-technical lock-in favoring major crops (Figure 3.6). [SOURCE: Page_99]

First, climate change can reinforce the lock-in situation. The increased frequency of biotic and abiotic stresses can lead to an increased probability of crop failure, especially for farmers unfamiliar with grain legume cultivation. Frequent failures due to climate change can thus hinder the introduction of these species in cropping systems by farmers, as described by Meynard et al. (2018). Farmers’ lack of interest in grain legumes may, in turn, reinforce barriers at other stages of the value chain (e.g., reduced investment in breeding and R&D, increased logistical constraints for collection and transformation). Second, the lock-in situation can hinder the process of adaptation to climate change. Lower investment in research and development compared with major species has left little margin for adaptation. For example, our interviewees perceived that weaker investment in breeding and public research’s disinvestment from minor species had resulted in slower genetic improvement compared to cereals, especially when it came the tolerance of these species to abiotic stresses (Table 3.1). A lack of technical references and knowledge may also prevent farmers from adapting their management practices to climate change. Finally, lack of market opportunities and stakeholder coordination, which require time to be designed, can hinder the short-term adoption of transformative adaptation strategies such as intercropping. [SOURCE: Page_99]

Conversely, climate change may contribute to a certain extent to unlocking the situation around grain legumes. As mentioned in several interviews, climate change may indeed foster a growing interest in cropping systems that include grain legumes (e.g., incentive-based environmental policies renewing interest in grain legumes, renewed interest in crop diversification to increase system robustness), and thus create new market opportunities for [SOURCE: Page_99]

98 [SOURCE: Page_99]

these crops. Climate change may also be an opportunity for new species, such as chickpea or soybean, in areas where they were not grown heretofore. [SOURCE: Page_100]

The socio-technical lock-in hindering the development of grain legumes has been well described in the literature. For the first time, we include climate change as an additional component of this lock-in – as well as an opportunity for unlocking. [SOURCE: Page_100]

Public policies can play a key role in unlocking the situation, as several authors have demonstrated (Balázs et al., 2021a, 2021b; Kuhlman et al., 2017; Zander et al., 2016). They are also central in climate change adaptation (Biesbroek et al., 2013; Eriksen et al., 2015). Public policies could therefore be a lever to promote the development of grain legumes in the context of climate change. However, it may require policy instruments to be redesigned to take into account the numerous challenges facing the development of grain legumes, as well as stakeholders’ specific needs. While the European Commission has already called for new policy instruments to be designed to increase protein crop production (European Parliament, 2011), the issue of ensuring legumes’ sustainability in the context of climate change has yet to be addressed. [SOURCE: Page_100]

Figure 3.6: Interaction between climate change and the socio-technical lock-in hindering grain legume development. Rightward arrows symbolize the impact of non-climatic barriers on the adaptation process, whereas leftward arrows symbolize the impact of climate change on the lock- in. Open circle arrows represent climatic barriers to the adaptation process. Data from the interviews are displayed in italics. [SOURCE: Page_100]

### 3.3.4. Filling knowledge gaps to support stakeholders’ adaptation

In the interviews, we identified a knowledge gap on grain legume response to climate change. Over one-quarter (27%) of the interviewees used information about climate change in their activities, but only few (12%) had access to information specifically about the impact of climate change on grain legumes. When asked how information about grain legume response to climate change could be useful to facilitate adaption, respondents provided a variety of potential applications. The nature of the information and the space and time scales for action depended on the intended recipient of the information (Table 3.2). [SOURCE: Page_101]

Table 3.2 : Potential use of information about the impact of climate change on grain legumes identified by the interviewees [SOURCE: Page_101]

Potential use           Nature of the               Potential         Space and time information                  users                scales [SOURCE: Page_101]

Identifying         Agroclimatic indicators         Local extension   Local scale adaptation          Climate analogues               services,         (département → plot) options                                             cooperatives      5-10 years [SOURCE: Page_101]

Guiding crop        Agroclimatic indicators         Seed breeders     National or European breeding            Climate analogues                                 scale programs                                                              10-30 years [SOURCE: Page_101]

Shifting crop       Suitability or yield maps       Extension         National scale → production          Maps of limiting factors        services,         supply area areas                                               cooperatives,     5-20 years industries [SOURCE: Page_101]

Choosing the        Information on yield and        Local extension   Local scale right grain         yield stability                 services,         (département → plot) legume species      Comparison with other           producers         5-10 years crops [SOURCE: Page_101]

In addition to this knowledge gap on grain legume response to climate change, some interviewees identified other knowledge gaps hindering adaptation. According to them, many farmers lacked knowledge and know-how on grain legume cultivation, which could either prevent adaptation (e.g., a lack of knowledge on the cultivation and the economic performances of new species could prevent their adoption by farmers) or lead to poor management and reduce the efficiency of adaptation (a lack of knowledge on winter crop cultivation, for example, would lead to poor disease management and lower yields when switching to winter crops). In addition, some interviewees from extension services and cooperatives mentioned a lack of knowledge on grain legume physiology to design adaptation options (e.g., knowledge about grain legume response to irrigation, about their pests and pathogens and the crop management that could help to prevent their emergence or spread). Some breeders highlighted a need for more collaboration between the breeding industry and [SOURCE: Page_101]

academic research to circulate knowledge on the genes enhancing stress tolerance in grain legumes. It is worth noting that almost one-third of the interviewees (27%) mentioned a lack of solutions to manage the biotic pressure, which is expected to increase with climate change. [SOURCE: Page_102]

Several studies have demonstrated the key role of knowledge in supporting grain legume adoption by farmers (Zimmer et al., 2016) and their adaptation to climate change (Howden et al., 2007). Our results show that stakeholders can be interested in specific knowledge about the impact of climate change on grain legumes. Such knowledge can be obtained through modelling, for example. However, the systematic review of model-based studies has highlighted a lack of data on grain legume response to climate change in Europe and a mismatch between available data and stakeholders’ needs (Marteau-Bazouni et al., 2024). To bridge the gap between knowledge and action for adaptation, participatory modelling should be explored as a tool to meet stakeholders’ needs and assist them in adapting to climate change (Beveridge et al., 2018; Naulleau et al., 2022). [SOURCE: Page_102]

Knowledge production does not solve everything, however. In examining how uncertainty impacts farmers’ decision-making, Singh et al. (2020) draw a distinction between cases where reducing uncertainty by increasing the knowledge on and understanding of a problem may help them to adapt, and cases characterized by high levels of observed variability that require other forms of support. Innovative design may be necessary to meet the numerous challenges associated with the adaptation of grain legumes to climate change (Prost et al., 2017). [SOURCE: Page_102]
